Setting `RECOMP_TRACE_BUDGET=0` disables those trace messages. This corrects the comparison method and makes the earlier zero splash-timer values from a USB/event stack sample non-diagnostic for the main thread.

With tracing disabled, a fresh 90-second watchdog run sampled the main guest thread inside the splash draw/fade path (`sub_000DFE75` / `sub_000E0231`). Guest clock advanced by about 66.3 seconds during the 90-second host interval; the fade timer was about 0.5 with a 0.05 increment. USER DMA `PUT=0xF3A8`, `GET=0xE910` confirms the queue continued advancing. The guest had loaded CAPCOM, Nude Maker, and Dolby logo assets, BGM and `ADVANCE.BIN`; the framebuffer captures still showed CAPCOM. The renderer reported 27 flips and about 0.44 guest FPS near the end, with 52 draw batches, 104 triangles, and about 16 million pixel writes. These figures show very poor startup presentation cadence in this partial renderer; they are not a stable performance benchmark, and they do not prove the rasterizer is the sole bottleneck. The OHCI model served virtual reports, but controller response remains unverified. No title screen, audible output, saves, or gameplay was verified.

A separate 45-second probe used the renderer's maximum 16-worker setting and reported 0.38 guest FPS at its last periodic report, with nine flips at that point; the saved final framebuffer still showed CAPCOM. This short run is not a controlled enough comparison to conclude whether the worker setting changes throughput. No title screen was reached. Keep all progress bars and Milestone 3 unchanged. ### 2026-10-18 follow-up: software-rasterizer timing sample

Temporary QPC instrumentation around `raster_batch()` was added to the ignored local XboxRecomp source for a watchdog-bounded 45-second run, then removed by restoring the pre-instrumentation file byte-for-byte. The renderer reported 0.38 guest FPS at nine flips. The run remained on the CAPCOM splash and did not verify the Steel Battalion title screen, audible output, physical-controller input, saves, or gameplay. ### 2026-10-18 follow-up: graphics-path wiring audit

The local runtime contains a Windows D3D8 COM compatibility layer backed by D3D11, and the umbrella `xboxrecomp` target links it. However, the observed title NV2A pushbuffer path is separate: `nv2a_pb_scan.c` decodes commands and, when `RECOMP_PB_EXEC` is enabled, dispatches them to `nv2a_pb_exec_method()` in `kernel/nv2a_pb_exec.c`, whose current visible-pixel path uses the CPU software rasterizer. The D3D8 bridge does not automatically receive this pushbuffer workload.

There is also an NV2A PGRAPH-to-D3D11 method translator in `src/nv2a/nv2a_pgraph_d3d11.c`. The current source documents it as a 5-dword inline-vertex, textured 2D menu profile; `pgraph_d3d11_init()` is called by the pushbuffer replay harness, while the normal scan path described above routes to the separate executor. A method-coverage audit is needed before judging whether this translator can be extended for Steel Battalion or whether a broader PGRAPH backend is required. Do not equate the existence of the D3D11 library with GPU-accelerated rendering of the game's current pushbuffer stream, and do not select or implement a replacement architecture yet. The exact `raster_batch()` timing above remains the measured performance evidence. Milestone 3 and all progress bars remain unchanged.

A source-to-trace comparison shows why the PGRAPH translator is not a drop-in renderer for the observed Steel Battalion startup workload. The 90-second guest run reported 52 vertex-program batches / 208 vertices with vertex attributes `v0` through `v15` in the observed attribute format. The executor also saw texture state at guest offset `0x803DF800` in 51 batches, but reported zero batches with a usable texture stage, so this is texture-state evidence rather than proof of successful texture sampling. The translator hardcodes five dwords per vertex (`X, Y, U, V, Color`) and its generic build path disables texturing and uses diffuse vertex color; a source search found no `GAME_HAS_FONT_ATLAS` definition in the local runtime checkout. Its optional texture mapping is a fixed reference-title lookup. The current executor separately interprets guest vertex programs and reports 155 distinct methods unhandled during startup; that count does not mean all 155 are visually significant. Any GPU path must preserve the game's vertex-program, texture, blend, and surface behavior, rather than forwarding this workload to the narrow menu translator. Next compare reuse options for the existing NV2A PGRAPH execution/shader code and define a semantics-preserving GPU raster backend; no backend switch or implementation is made by this audit.

### 2026-10-18 follow-up: renderer reuse and license boundary

The checked-out local XboxRecomp tree (`v0.13.1-dirty`) identifies the toolkit as MIT, with explicit LGPL-2.1-or-later exceptions for `src/apu/` and `src/nv2a/nv2a_regs.h`. Its contribution guidance specifically says not to port GPL code from Cxbx-Reloaded or the GPL portions of xemu. Cxbx-Reloaded publishes under GPL-2.0, and xemu's top-level `COPYING` is GPL version 2. Therefore, directly porting either project's PGRAPH renderer is not compatible with the current upstream contribution policy and would require a deliberate licensing review before any reuse. No code was copied and no project license was changed.

The viable research paths are to implement a semantics-preserving hardware raster backend against the existing decoded NV2A state using original code, or to pursue an explicitly approved GPL-compatible integration after reviewing project-wide distribution obligations and the XboxRecomp upstream policy. Continue with original-code feasibility and method-coverage analysis first; do not begin a GPL-derived port or change the license implicitly. Milestone 3 and all progress bars remain unchanged.

At this earlier checkpoint, the option comparison in [`GRAPHICS_BACKEND_OPTIONS.md`](GRAPHICS_BACKEND_OPTIONS.md) recommended D3D12 and listed Vulkan as an alternative. That recommendation has since been superseded by the final Vulkan-only decision at the top of this status page and in the current backend decision record.

The bridge served 451 periodic transfers by the 45-second watchdog sample, and the synthetic Start source was active. No XID `GET_CAPABILITIES` request appeared in this trace, and there is still no evidence that the game logic acted on Start. This verifies that the virtual endpoint matches the requested Steel Battalion report size and is being polled, but not complete controller compatibility, output/light behavior, or gameplay. Captures and trace remain local under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-usb-xid-trace-45s-20261018/`; no progress bars change. Next determine why the guest proceeds from XID descriptor retrieval to input polling without requesting capabilities, while continuing the splash-to-title trace.

A matched 90-second run with the same build and Steel Battalion input profile again ended at the watchdog with a CAPCOM logo in the last sampled guest framebuffer. The latest GPU sample reported 0.43 guest FPS at 27 flips; this is an early startup measurement, not a gameplay benchmark. OHCI counted 2,444 periodic transfers, including 2,434 input reports and 10 non-control OUT transfers. Source inspection shows the current OHCI bridge counts non-control OUT transfers but accepts and discards their payloads; the initial stats-only trace did not record those bytes, so their function was then unknown. This is a concrete gap for future device-output work, not proof that those packets control indicator lights. The Steel Battalion title screen remains unverified. Local logs and captures are under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-usb-startup-90s-20261018/`; progress bars remain unchanged. The payload trace follow-up is recorded below.

A temporary, local-only OHCI trace then captured non-control OUT payloads during another bounded 90-second run. The guest sent 22-byte packets on endpoint 1. Nonzero fields changed in byte 3's high nibble and byte 18's low nibble; the public [XboxDevWiki Steel Battalion XID table](https://xboxdevwiki.net/Xbox_Input_Devices) labels those positions `Start` and `GearN`. This is consistent with the game updating controller status/indicator channels, but their hardware meaning has not been confirmed on a physical controller. The current bridge discards these writes, so original-controller output functionality remains absent. The temporary trace change was restored byte-for-byte to source SHA-256 `21E55855E52C34CBAB1755B72981324CC51D31C62BDA8B2B0F513327369ED3A7`, and the normal Release target rebuilt successfully. Raw packet captures remain local and ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-usb-out-payload-90s-20261018/`. Progress bars remain unchanged. Next validate output channels with a physical Steel Battalion controller/adapter or an independently documented test, then implement a narrow output-forwarding path.

A matched 180-second run with the virtual Steel Battalion profile, synthetic Start pulses, USB stats, async file I/O, and pushbuffer execution advanced the 640×480 guest framebuffer from CAPCOM to the Nude Maker logo by capture 12. The watchdog stack still sampled `sub_000DFE75`, the startup logo draw/fade path; the final NV2A USER DMA PUT/GET were `0x1BD20` / `0x1B270`. The latest periodic GPU sample was 0.43 guest FPS at 69 flips, and OHCI served 5,512 periodic transfers (5,491 input reports, 21 non-control OUT packets). This is repeatable splash progression with active virtual input, not a title screen or gameplay. The run ended at its watchdog with no remaining game process. Artifacts stay local under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-usb-startup-180s-20261018/`. Progress bars remain unchanged; next determine the subsequent splash/title transition and keep accuracy and rendering work behind the existing backend approval gate.

A five-minute run with the same build and USB profile advanced the guest framebuffer to the Dolby Digital logo by capture 20, after the previously verified CAPCOM and Nude Maker logos. At the watchdog the guest stack still pointed into the logo draw/fade path; NV2A USER DMA PUT/GET were `0x2D0C0` / `0x2C628`. OHCI served 9,669 periodic transfers (9,627 input reports, 42 non-control OUT packets). The latest GPU sample reported 0.43 guest FPS at 117 flips. The watchdog ended the process after 300 seconds; no title screen, native-window presentation, audio, saves, or gameplay was verified. This confirms startup logo progression under the repeated Start probe but exposes extremely slow startup rendering. Artifacts remain local under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-usb-startup-300s-20261018/`; all progress bars remain unchanged. The next useful boot step is to trace the guest's post-Dolby state transition and address the renderer bottleneck only within the approved graphics architecture.

Static XBE call-graph analysis narrowed the five-minute checkpoint: startup function `sub_000467A0` calls `sub_000E0200`, and only after that returns does it enter its `sub_000E3510` event-dispatch loop. `sub_000E0200` invokes `sub_000DFF40` twice for its logo helpers. The 300-second watchdog stack contains return address `0x000E0362` from the second helper call (`sub_000DFF40` argument 2), with the draw path below it in `sub_000DFBC0`; it has not returned to the event dispatcher. The captured framebuffer shows Dolby at this checkpoint. This establishes that startup remains inside the logo routine, but does not distinguish an intentional wait, a missed guest-side input condition, or severe rendering delay. The next probe should record the logo helper's loop/timer/input condition and return edge, without patching guest behavior.

### 2026-10-18 watchdog instruction-pointer correction

A closer decode of the five-minute guest stack resolves the sampled instruction pointer `0x000DFE75` inside `sub_000DFBC0` (function range `0x000DFBC0–0x000DFE83`), the draw routine reached from `sub_000DFF40`; return address `0x000E0362` is the caller continuation after the second logo-helper call. This is a point-in-time guest PC inside the draw call, not evidence that a timer or input wait is the active instruction. In the same run, the GPU report had only 117 flips after 300 seconds, and the isolated raster timing probe measured 2.18–2.40 seconds in `raster_batch()` for each sampled splash frame. Together these observations make CPU software rasterization the leading measured startup bottleneck, while not proving it is the only cause of the missing title screen.

The earlier call-path note's alternatives (intentional timer wait or missed input condition) remain possible at other times but are not supported as the active watchdog location. At this historical checkpoint the next-renderer proposal was an incremental D3D12 backend. That proposal is superseded; the final project renderer is Vulkan only. Do not claim boot until the title screen is repeatable. No feature bars advance.

### Native GPU API selection and prototype status (2026-10-18)

Historical backend assessment (superseded): an earlier checkpoint recommended native Direct3D 12 based on Windows integration and CPU submission goals, while noting that no matched Steel Battalion benchmark proved it faster than Vulkan. The project decision is now fixed to Vulkan; this historical note does not authorize another backend change. The underlying measured opportunity remains CPU-side rendering cost (2.18–2.40 seconds per sampled splash frame).

A local-only D3D12 flat-triangle prototype was added to the ignored XboxRecomp checkout and the Windows x64 target builds successfully. The prototype is opt-in through `RECOMP_D3D12_RASTER=1`, retains the software reference path, and only attempts flat untextured draws with blending, depth, and alpha testing disabled. This is not yet a useful acceleration of the observed splash workload: the traced first two startup draws use depth, and the second is textured, so they fall outside its supported state. No runtime image-parity test, performance comparison, or D3D12 coverage of the measured slow path has been verified. The current synchronous full-surface upload/readback design must not be treated as a performance result. Progress bars remain unchanged; no title screen is verified. Next redesign around batched NV2A work and GPU-resident surfaces, then compare identical guest frames and timings against the software renderer.

The Windows x64 target built successfully and the ordinary software raster self-test produced the expected magenta triangle in the 640×480 guest framebuffer. ### D3D12 PSO fix and guest-surface smoke check (2026-10-18)

The earlier `E_INVALIDARG` was isolated to the scalar pixel-shader input signature. The minimal no-input and POSITION-only probes created PSOs; the full pipeline succeeded when the interpolated color was carried as a structured `TEXCOORD` between vertex and pixel shaders. All temporary probe variants were removed. After a fresh Release x64 rebuild, a bounded launch with `RECOMP_D3D12_RASTER=1` and `RECOMP_RASTER_TEST=1` logged `[D3D12-R] first flat triangle completed at 640x480` and `[GPU] D3D12 raster self-test passed`; the captured 640×480 guest framebuffer shows the expected magenta triangle. This verifies native D3D12 device/queue/PSO creation, a basic draw, and readback into guest memory. The diagnostic run was externally bounded and left no game process. Artifacts stay local and ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-d3d12-final-selftest-20261018/`. Progress bars remain unchanged. Next implement a batched stateful path for the actual startup draw, preserving the software renderer as the comparison reference.

A paired software-reference run disabled `RECOMP_D3D12_RASTER` but kept the same synthetic triangle and launch settings. Its BMP SHA-256 exactly matches the D3D12-enabled capture (`839BB69098BBFCB9A63CC0EED0E66DF99EC18EBF174D7F98903FE07AC69CD5ED`). This verifies byte-identical output for the synthetic flat triangle only; it is not a comparison of the game's textured/depth-enabled draws or a performance measurement.

### D3D12 actual startup strip checkpoint (2026-10-18)

The Release x64 target rebuilt successfully. A bounded launch with pushbuffer execution, synthetic AC'97 readiness, async I/O, virtual Steel Battalion USB, repeated Start pulses, and DSP acknowledgement unset activated the opt-in D3D12 path on the first measured guest draw. The matcher was corrected to the captured four-vertex triangle strip, not a quad. The runtime logged native D3D12 initialization, a six-vertex D3D12 batch at 640×480, and completion of the actual opaque-black depth-tested startup pass. This verifies the renderer accepts that specific guest draw state and writes through the experimental full-surface upload/readback route.

The first comparison used periodic framebuffer dumps and was inconclusive. Inspection showed the existing after-draw hook only covered fixed-function raster batches; transformed NV2A draws bypassed it. Added an opt-in capture directly after transformed draws and suppressed periodic dumps during that mode. Matching first-draw captures exposed a real D3D12 error: its vertex-buffer view declared room for three vertices although the strip batch submitted six, so the second triangle read outside the declared view. Changed the view size to the submitted vertex count and rebuilt successfully.

After the fix, the first two 640×480 captures taken at transformed-draw completion are byte-identical between D3D12 and the software reference. Both D3D12 captures and both software captures have SHA-256 `9DC5CDCF2B4ABD98A266B99496FA0062BEC2C56E30B979A7CB5D033275EED1B3`. This verifies parity for the captured startup black pass and following transparent textured pass only. It does not establish title-screen rendering, complete NV2A correctness, audio, gameplay, frame pacing, or a speedup. The current path synchronously uploads and reads back the full surface, so it remains unsuitable as the performance architecture. Logs/captures are ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-sync-draw-d3d12-vbv-fix-20261018/` and `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-sync-draw-software-20261018/`. Progress bars remain unchanged. Next extend correctness to a meaningful visible textured pass, then move to GPU-resident batching before measuring throughput.

### No-ack startup draw state reproduced (2026-10-18)

A 180-second bounded run used RECOMP_PB_EXEC=1, RECOMP_AC97_READY=1, RECOMP_ASYNC_IO=1, the virtual Steel Battalion USB profile, repeated Start pulses, and frame tracing. DSP acknowledgement remained unset. The run exited at the external bound; no project process remains. It reproduced the slow startup path: at the later sample the guest had three flips and four draw calls (eight triangles); GPU samples remained about 0.09 FPS on the brief intervals. The first two traced passes each cover roughly the 640x480 surface and use depth test LEQUAL with depth writes.

The first pass is untextured and unblended. All three sampled vertices have screen-space z=0, w=1, and d0=(0,0,0,1); combiner state is stage program 0, final0 0x200C0000, final1 0x00001C80. Evaluating that state through the current CPU combiner yields opaque black. The second pass samples a 512x512 DXT1 texture from guest offset 0x803DF800, blends with SRC_ALPHA / ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA, and has constant vertex d0 RGB 0.690196 with alpha 0 in this sample. These are actual guest draw inputs, not a D3D12 parity result. The process still does not reach the title screen; actual audible output and gameplay remain unverified. The trace and guest-derived data remain local/ignored at iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-draw-repro-180s-20261018/.

The temporary vertex-output trace was removed from the ignored runtime source; a fresh Release x64 rebuild succeeded with existing warnings. Next implement and compare a batched D3D12 pass that can preserve depth and the observed combiner/texture/blend semantics. Do not use the current synchronous per-triangle full-surface upload/readback prototype as the performance architecture. Keep all progress bars unchanged.

This earlier Vulkan recommendation is superseded by the Windows D3D12 decision below. At that point, renderer maturity was considered, but no direct API benchmark was available.

The D3D12 prototype remains a correctness probe; its synchronous surface upload/readback is unsuitable for performance, and startup parity is limited to two splash passes. The software rasterizer remains the pixel reference.

No matching D3D12/Vulkan benchmark exists for this game's captures, and no renderer performance result was verified. This historical integration proposal was superseded by the D3D12 recommendation and is now superseded by the fixed Vulkan decision.

### Vulkan integration feasibility audit (2026-10-18)

Read-only inspection of xemu master `8fc3208` found 17 C translation units in `hw/xbox/nv2a/pgraph/vk/meson.build`; source files carry LGPL-2.1-or-later notices. The wrap files pin Volk (MIT), glslang (mixed BSD and additional component licenses), VMA (MIT), and SPIRV-Reflect (Apache-2.0) at exact commits now recorded in `docs/GRAPHICS_BACKEND_OPTIONS.md`. The Windows Vulkan headers/loader source is not pinned there. This remains a preliminary license inventory: audit compiled glslang pieces, transitive build dependencies/notices, and Windows runtime packaging before selecting the repository license or distributing binaries. Renderer functions are tightly coupled to xemu `NV2AState`/`PGRAPHState`, QEMU helper and memory-region APIs, shared xemu PGRAPH/surface/texture structures, and an offscreen GL context used for optional external-memory sharing.

The ignored XboxRecomp checkout has only a minimal PGRAPH state stub and a narrow D3D11 method handler. The Vulkan renderer cannot be linked in as-is. A port must map the real pushbuffer executor's guest state into a fuller NV2A/PGRAPH model or place the Vulkan renderer behind a new adapter contract, and must account for QEMU utility substitutions and the optional GL interop path. This is substantial engineering, not a hard dead end; it does not require the emulator CPU/kernel/machine loop. No source was copied, no build/test was run, and no milestone progress changed. The initial adapter seam is now documented: keep pushbuffer decoding and the current CPU VSH transform, send clipped geometry plus explicit render/surface/texture state to a Vulkan raster backend, preserve CPU raster as reference, and synchronize guest RAM only at true visibility boundaries. A read-only `vulkaninfo --summary` probe verified the installed loader sees the RTX 4070 SUPER (API 1.4.351, driver 617.42) and reports fragment-shader interlock, custom-border-color, external-memory-host, and dynamic-rendering extensions. No Vulkan SDK environment or development headers are configured. Next compare renderer extraction with a smaller Vulkan raster module, audit the exact build dependencies, and resolve a reproducible Windows Vulkan SDK/loader source without touching user ISO/game data.

The Vulkan integration route was not selected because its license closure remains unresolved and no comparative speed advantage was found. Milestone 3 and all bars remain unchanged.

### Guest splash helper exit analysis (2026-10-18)

Static review of the local-only XBE analysis and generated translation confirms that startup function `sub_000E0200` calls two `sub_000DFF40` logo helpers in order. Each helper draws while advancing the guest fade timer by 0.05 after a group of 11 draw-loop iterations, and its loop exits at the configured timer threshold. The caller proceeds to its event-dispatch path only after both helpers return. A prior five-minute watchdog sample placed the guest PC in the logo draw routine, with return address `0x000E0362`, immediately after the second helper call; that sample therefore had not yet returned to the event loop. This is consistent with the newer captures showing the Dolby splash after five minutes and about 0.43 guest FPS. The timing cost is a likely reason startup remains in the splash sequence, but it is not a verified fix or proof of the only cause. No input shortcut or guest behavior change was made, and the XBE-derived C remains local/ignored. Next verify the second helper's return and event-loop entry with a controlled longer run, or proceed with the approved GPU acceleration route when its license boundary is resolved. No progress bars advance.

### D3D12 renderer reuse audit (2026-10-18)

Read the current XboxXBERecomp D3D12 source and its API/license manifests without cloning it into the repository. Its renderer is a large native D3D12 backend driven through normalized XDK D3D8 state; it is not a raw NV2A PGRAPH packet consumer. It is MIT-licensed at the repository level and lists toml++, Dear ImGui, and SDL 3 in `THIRD_PARTY.txt`, but the renderer depends on that project’s app, window, diagnostics, and kernel APIs. Local XboxRecomp still has a distinct D3D8-to-D3D11 path; its `dev_BeginPush` and `dev_EndPush` return `E_NOTIMPL`. Steel Battalion startup is currently observed entering the independent low-level `nv2a_pb_exec` path. This makes the renderer a valuable state/resource-management reference, not a drop-in replacement for the observed pushbuffer path. No upstream code was copied and no local runtime source was changed.

The then-proposed D3D12 interface work is historical and superseded by Vulkan. Keep XboxRecomp's static-recompilation base and do not advance graphics enhancement work before title boot and rendering parity are achieved. Milestone 3 and progress bars remain unchanged.

### D3D12 splash-path bounded measurement (2026-10-18)

Ran the existing Release x64 executable for 120 seconds with the neutral Steel Battalion USB profile, the same pushbuffer/async-I/O/AC'97/vblank/title-event diagnostics as the matched splash runs, and `RECOMP_D3D12_RASTER=1`. No build was run. The D3D12 path initialized and completed the specific opaque-black, fullscreen depth-tested quad; all other draws continued through the CPU software rasterizer. By the external bound the guest still showed the CAPCOM splash, with 39 flips and about 0.43 FPS in the final sample, comparable to the prior software-only ~0.44 FPS matched run. This does not show a measurable performance gain. It confirms the current D3D12 implementation covers too narrow a state pattern to accelerate the actual splash sequence. The log also had no D3D8 `[D3D]` periodic Present counter lines while the raw pushbuffer flip count advanced, supporting that the observed rendering path is not currently being driven through the upstream-style high-level D3D8 backend. No title screen, audio output, or controller gameplay was verified. Process was externally stopped at 120 seconds and no game process remained. Logs/captures are ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-d3d12-splash-120s-20261018/`. ### Symbolized boot watchdog sample (2026-10-18)

Ran the current Release x64 build for 35 seconds with D3D12 enabled and `RECOMP_WATCHDOG_NATIVE_PC=1`. The bounded watchdog exited with code 3 and no game process remained. Its symbolized host stack was `sub_00211E10` → `sub_0020E520` → `sub_000DFBC0` → `sub_000E0200`; the captured guest return address was `0x000DFE75`, inside the original logo helper. `sub_00211E10` contains a guest-side linked-list scan at `0x00211F10`. This sample places execution inside translated game code during logo drawing, rather than in a D3D12 wait/readback function. One stack sample does not show how much time this loop consumes or establish a fault in the translation.

Next quantify repeated samples or loop iterations around the guest `sub_00211E10` linked-list path while preserving the original code and splash timing. Do not patch generated XBE code or skip the logo sequence. Keep the software rasterizer as reference; broader GPU-resident batching remains deferred until boot-path timing identifies its contribution. Milestone 3 and all progress bars remain unchanged. Local trace output is ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-watchdog-stack-35s-20261018/` and generated game code remains ignored under `iso/local-analysis/`.

A second 35-second watchdog run without native symbolization produced the same guest stack return address `0x000DFE75`, with the same saved caller frames through `0x0020E5C0` and `0x0021B790`. This independently confirms the splash helper call chain is still active at the sample time. A 20-second repeat with native symbolization did not finish its report: the process remained alive with the guest thread suspended and its log unchanged, so it was stopped. This exposes a hang risk in the optional DbgHelp symbolization path; do not use that option for further repeated sampling until the watchdog helper is bounded or fixed. The ordinary guest-stack watchdog completed normally with exit code 3. Neither run reached the title screen. Logs remain ignored in `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-watchdog-guest-stack-35s-20261018/` and `m3-watchdog-stack-20s-20261018/`.

### Logo wait loop identified as an NV2A semaphore wait (2026-10-18)

Temporarily instrumented the ignored generated `sub_00211E10` translation to count loop iterations and sample the address it reads. Several calls looped on the unchanged dword at `0x80003000`, with scans reaching 580,000,001 and 596,534,849 iterations before the value advanced and the guest function returned. This is a polling wait, not a linked-list traversal. The generated XBE source was restored byte-for-byte from its local backup after the probe, and the clean Release target rebuilt successfully; no instrumentation or game-derived code was staged.

Then enabled the pushbuffer semaphore trace on the restored build. The executor resolved semaphore handle `0x8`, instance `0x11B0`, offset `0`, physical `0x3000`, guest VA `0x80003000`, and logged `BACK_END_WRITE_SEMAPHORE_RELEASE` values progressing from `5` through `0x17`. This is the producer of the exact word polled by `sub_00211E10`; the guest wait is tracking NV2A command completion. This rules out a missing kernel event as the immediate cause of the sampled wait. The executor does release the semaphore, but slow guest-visible progress remains consistent with the low software-renderer throughput and the narrow D3D12 coverage. It does not prove that rendering is the only limiting work.

Next measure and improve complete NV2A batch throughput while preserving semaphore ordering. The current D3D12 path still uploads and reads back the full surface synchronously; a GPU-resident path requires a coherence audit for CPU reads/writes, software fallbacks, and flips before changing that behavior. Keep the software path as reference; do not alter guest semaphore values or bypass the wait. Milestone 3 and every progress bar remain unchanged. Local captures/logs and the temporary generated-code backup stay ignored in `iso/local-analysis/`.

### xemu shader-generator license boundary clarified (2026-10-18)

A pinned-source review found that xemu's Vulkan PGRAPH target is not solely an LGPL renderer path. The Vulkan shader cache generates vertex and pixel GLSL on cache misses; the called vertex-program generator and pixel/register-combiner generator carry GPL-2-or-3 notices and identify Cxbx/Dxbx/Xeon as prior sources. The Vulkan compiler wrapper also directly invokes glslang preprocessing, parsing, linking, and SPIR-V generation. These are required runtime paths in xemu's design, not unused files. See docs/VULKAN_REUSE_AUDIT.md for pinned source links and file-level findings.

Consequently, do not copy the complete xemu Vulkan renderer and describe it as LGPL-only. Before reuse, either clear a compatible project-wide/dependency license path, or replace the GPL shader-generation components with independently authored code and complete the remaining glslang/QEMU include audit. The existing project-owned CPU VSH and combiner implementations cite xemu behavior as reference; their provenance also needs review before distribution. No xemu code was copied or new runtime code added in this research update. The Vulkan backend remains locked, and no progress bars or Milestone 3 status change.

The preceding exact opaque-black/depth quad is reproduced with a CPU rectangle/depth fill. The Vulkan path reads its result back into the guest surface so later legacy CPU draws observe the same memory; unsupported states still use the CPU reference. Removed the D3D12 raster branches from the active pushbuffer executor. Vulkan remains the only host GPU direction.

DXC 1.9.2609 compiled the project-owned HLSL to SPIR-V embedded as C arrays; the DXC tool and intermediate binaries are local only. The Release x64 target built successfully through the installed Visual Studio Build Tools. Its log is ignored at `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-vulkan-textured-logo-seeded-120s-20261018/`.

The run proves that the narrow Vulkan submission path executes, not that its pixels match the CPU renderer. No framebuffer capture was taken; logo orientation/color parity, later draw ordering, performance improvement, title-screen boot, audio, controller response, or campaign play remain unverified. Keep all progress bars and Milestone 3 status unchanged. Next compare a captured Vulkan frame against the existing CPU reference and capture a guest stack at the `0x54A8` stall before expanding this path to other draws.

### Vulkan draw-path timing investigation (2026-10-18)

Added temporary, opt-in local timing around pushbuffer segments, NV2A method handlers, and draw batches. A corrected 30-second watchdog run selected the RTX 4070 SUPER, initialized the 640×480 Vulkan swapchain, and presented the first image. It produced three local 640×480 captures and exited at watchdog code 3; the Steel Battalion title screen was not reached. At the watchdog, USER DMA PUT was `0x5F40` and GET was `0x54A8`, so the guest had submitted more work than the compatibility worker had completed.

The slow segment `0x4F5C–0x54A8` took 2.453 seconds. Its four `SET_BEGIN_END` calls accounted for 2.340 seconds. Draw-level timing separated that work: two four-vertex textured batches fell through to the CPU reference rasterizer and took 1.137 and 1.165 seconds; the narrowly matched Vulkan logo batch took 37 ms, and the opaque-black depth quad took 9 ms. This is direct evidence that the remaining generic CPU draw fallback is the dominant bottleneck in this startup sample. It does not establish stable frame rate, general Vulkan rendering correctness, or title-screen compatibility. The timing code, executable, captures, and logs remain local and ignored; no game-derived material is tracked.

The corrected build succeeded, and its watchdog run produced the measurements above. No progress bars advance. ### USB XID request sequence during startup (2026-10-18)

A separate 120-second Vulkan run enabled OHCI request tracing with the Steel Battalion profile. The runtime returned all requested bytes. The control trace recorded no USB stalls and no `GET_CAPABILITIES` request. OHCI then completed 464 periodic transfers before the watchdog (370 input reports, 94 other endpoint transfers, 6 setup packets).

The descriptor values and report layout align with the [documented Steel Battalion XID descriptor and report table](https://xboxdevwiki.net/Xbox_Input_Devices). The locally captured request/response sequence is stronger evidence of guest-side enumeration than periodic packet counts alone. ### Steel Battalion input mapping cross-check (2026-10-18)

Cross-checked the title probe against the public [Xbox Input Devices reference](https://xboxdevwiki.net/Xbox_Input_Devices#Controller_to_Xbox): the 26-byte Steel Battalion report defines `RightJoyMainWeapon` at byte 2, mask `0x01`. The prior live probe delivered that exact bit in the press/release report, but the title framebuffer stayed unchanged. The report field and host-side A mapping therefore agree with the published table; game-side consumption is still unverified. Next inspect whether the title consumes XID input from control `GET_REPORT` or interrupt-IN transfers, and trace the guest's response path before changing the bit mapping.

The current XInput fallback is partial by design and is not complete controller support. It maps A/B/X to three right-joystick buttons, maps the two gamepad sticks to only some axes, and leaves the remaining switches, buttons, pedals, tuner, and `SightChangeY` unassigned. The original controller and its indicators have not been tested on physical hardware. Do not claim full Steel Battalion controller support or commit the speculative XInput mapping until guest response is verified.

### Guest input gate from static XPP path inspection (2026-10-18)

A follow-up read of generated guest code clarifies the controller path. `sub_00049DD0` clears global `0x351CB0`, calls `sub_00256BDE` to enumerate devices, iterates four device slots, and for each live handle calls `sub_00257103` to obtain state. The helper sets `0x351CB0` when a slot is recognized. Its callers `sub_0004A960` and `sub_0004AA80` call `sub_00049970` only when that global is nonzero. This is a conditional guest XInput-style path, separate from OHCI DMA delivery. The 320-second trace logged the first `sub_00049970` invocation but no 6,000-call checkpoint; it did not record the device mask, and the button-state log stayed silent. Thus the remaining unknown is whether device enumeration leaves the game-side gate active during the title prompt. Do not change controller mapping based on the report trace alone.

### Matched startup raster-worker comparison (2026-10-18)

Two matched 45-second startup runs used the same Release executable and `RECOMP_RASTER_THREADS` override, changing only the worker count. On this host (16 logical processors), the one-worker run recorded 10 timings for the same 512×512 textured CPU-raster draw (mean 801.43 ms, range 770.77–837.03 ms) and four framebuffer captures. The 16-worker run recorded eight timings (mean 1,279.92 ms, range 1,264.58–1,299.43 ms) and three captures. In these startup samples, 16 workers were about 60% slower for this draw. This result applies to the measured splash draw and does not establish performance for other scenes or gameplay.

The worker pool currently defaults to `GetSystemInfo().dwNumberOfProcessors - 4`, which yields 12 workers on this host; `RECOMP_RASTER_THREADS` is an existing runtime override. The comparison does not justify changing the default from one narrowly sampled draw. Keep the default unchanged until a broader set of draw workloads is profiled. The runs remained in startup; no title progression, gameplay, audio, or input result was verified. Logs are local/ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-raster-threads-{1,16}-20261018/`. Next: profile the distinct startup draw classes and determine whether time is dominated by CPU rasterization, asset loading, or guest-side work before another title progression attempt.

### Runtime verification of the title semaphore release (2026-10-18)

A 45-second bounded Release x64 run enabled the pushbuffer executor, full packet scan, and semaphore trace. The executor observed subchannel-0 `BACK_END_WRITE_SEMAPHORE_RELEASE` packets with values increasing from `0x5` to `0x2D`. Each traced packet resolved title handle 8 through instance `0x011B0` and its descriptor to guest VA `0x80003000` (physical `0x3000`); the stored value matched the submitted release value. This verifies that the current descriptor-based handler writes the target the guest polls, without a hardcoded game address.

The watchdog still found the guest waiting: registers were `EAX=8`, `ECX=0x2D`, `EDX=0x80003000`, `ESI=0x39`, `EDI=0x0C`; USER DMA PUT/GET were `0x8454`/`0x7470`. The queue completion value had advanced, but the guest remained 12 entries behind against its 8-entry threshold. The same run measured each 512x512 splash CPU-raster draw at about 720–730 ms and pushbuffer batches at about 2.45 seconds. This makes the slow draw path a strong contributor to queue lag, but does not prove it is the only cause. The run exited at watchdog code 3 and did not verify title presentation, audio, input response, or gameplay.

No progress bar advances from semaphore delivery alone. Next: profile the queue consumer and render workload together, then expand the accelerated draw path based on exact CPU-reference comparisons so the queue can drain at correct guest-visible completion points.

### OHCI ISR and guest DPC path (2026-10-19)

The USB controller is connected to guest interrupt vector 1 with ISR `0x0025BD47` and context `0x01081A18`. Static XBE data resolves the ISR's callback slot at `0x0025D278` to the kernel import `KeInsertQueueDpc`. The ISR queues guest DPC `0x0025C308` (`DPC=0x01081E58`, same context). A 35-second watchdog-bounded run traced eight enqueue calls for that USB DPC and seven executions before the watchdog; OHCI reported its WDH interrupt as claimed. The final OHCI sample showed 156 periodic transfers and 101 input reports. This confirms the interrupt-to-DPC path runs during startup.

Static inspection of `sub_0025C308` shows it reads HCCA `DoneHead` from controller context offset `+8+0x84`, clears the field, walks the completed descriptor list, and dispatches completion routines including `sub_0025C982` and `sub_0025BFA3`. This narrows the remaining gap to whether the interrupt-IN completion for the Steel Battalion report is traversed and how its guest buffer reaches the state returned by `sub_00257103`.

The configured A-button script was parsed but never logged a press before the 35-second watchdog, so this run is not an input test and did not reach a verified title screen. This verifies those startup images reached the presented framebuffer; it does not show the title prompt or verify input, sound output, or gameplay. The windowed process exited after the bounded run.

The captures are local and ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-windowed-fb-capture-60s-20261019/`; no copyrighted frame was added to GitHub. Milestone 3 and progress bars remain unchanged. Next inspect why the Steel Battalion screen remains very dim and determine whether the guest proceeds to its title prompt before testing input.

### Startup frame stability check (2026-10-19)

Comparing the five windowed framebuffer dumps from this particular run shows the first sample contains the Dolby splash and the next four samples—taken about ten seconds apart—are identical Steel Battalion logo frames. That established a period without visible change in that capture, but did not prove the startup was permanently stalled. A later correlated run showed several distinct startup logos across its captures before reaching a wait; see the follow-up below.

Next: correlate the frame sequence with the guest wait and event signal state in one run.

### Windowed startup and notification-event correlation (2026-10-19)

A repeated 90-second windowed run captured changing startup logo frames before the presentation remained on the Dolby screen in the last samples. The earlier set of four identical Steel Battalion-logo frames was a limited unchanged interval; it did not establish a permanent startup freeze. The run used the separate watchdog log so heavy stderr output could not hide the timeout report. The watchdog exited the process after 90 seconds.

The watchdog guest stack places the guest at return site `0x0020EC83`, waiting on event object `0x0021DCE4`; NV2A USER PUT and GET were equal at `0x0003F990`. A separate watch of that event's signal-state word `0x0021DCE8` observed transitions between 0 and 1 across concurrent kernel calls, including calls to the wait and set-event bridges. This does not establish whether the host wait was released and re-entered, or whether a wake was lost. It narrows the next trace to the host wait result and setter/waiter ordering for this exact notification event.

No title prompt, audio output, controller response, or gameplay was verified. Captures and logs remain local/ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-windowed-event-state-90s-20261019/`. Progress bars stay unchanged; Milestone 3 remains active.

### USB enumeration stopped before XID binding (2026-10-19)

Re-read the captured `stderr.log` as a sequence of OHCI setup transactions. It contains only `GET_DESCRIPTOR(DEVICE)`, `SET_ADDRESS`, `GET_DESCRIPTOR(CONFIGURATION)`, and `SET_CONFIGURATION`; no XID vendor `GET_DESCRIPTOR` or report request appears. OHCI statistics show the control endpoint descriptor, but no periodic-table entry is ever observed and the periodic transfer count remains zero. The runtime's configuration descriptor advertises the Steel Battalion interface and interrupt endpoints, and its USB profile has an XID descriptor handler, but this capture never exercises that handler. The evidence therefore places the input gap before interrupt scheduling: the guest USB/XID stack did not proceed to request or publish an interrupt endpoint in this run. The reason remains unknown, and this does not prove all game input uses this path.

No runtime source changed and no new build or launch was performed. This narrows the next step to identifying the guest or kernel path that binds an interface-class `0x58` XID device after configuration, then comparing the expected XID control requests to the four observed standard requests. Keep the virtual report profile and controller mapping unchanged until that path is established; game-side input remains unverified. Milestone 3 and progress bars remain unchanged.

### Queue-space wait and GPU interrupt gap (2026-10-19)

Static code review clarifies that event `0x0021DCF4` is used by generated guest helper `sub_00211E10` as a queue-space wake event. The helper compares the queue producer and completion values; when the outstanding distance reaches `0x2000`, it calls `KeWaitForSingleObject` on the event, then rechecks the counters after waking. The targeted trace's single wait-entry and absent return/set records match that path. The watchdog later found USER `PUT=GET=0x0006CBD0`, so the pushbuffer had drained by the sample, while the guest had not returned from the wait.

The current semaphore-release handler stores the guest completion value, but contains no interrupt delivery. The current runtime also explicitly leaves NV2A interrupt status registers at zero and its kernel bridge comment says no GPU interrupt is delivered. This is the leading explanation for why the guest can remain asleep after completion; it is still a hypothesis until the title's registered GPU ISR/DPC and the release-to-notification sequence are traced. It is separate from USB: USB enumeration did not publish a periodic endpoint, while this queue-space helper concerns the GPU command ring.

Next: identify the exact interrupt routine registered by the guest D3D runtime, determine which NV2A status/enable bits it expects for completion, then implement and verify hardware-equivalent notification through the existing interrupt path. Do not directly set the guest event or hardcode the Steel Battalion event address. No runtime source was changed or rebuilt for this static review. Milestone 3 and progress bars remain unchanged.

### Queue-event and vector-3 DPC distinction (2026-10-19)

A cross-check against the generated guest ISR/DPC corrects the scope of the previous interrupt hypothesis. The vector-3 DPC calls `sub_00216BE0`, which invokes the guest event-set import on `context + 0x194`. With the connected vector-3 context `0x0021DB50`, that is event `0x0021DCE4`, the neighboring notification event. The queue-space helper instead waits on `0x0021DCF4` (`0x0021B790 + 0x2564`). These are separate event objects with separate uses. Therefore, the absent GPU-interrupt delivery can explain a missed signal to `0x0021DCE4`, but it does not yet explain the `0x0021DCF4` wait recorded at the latest watchdog. The previous entry's GPU-interrupt diagnosis remains unproven for the queue-space stall.

Next inspect the owner/producer path for `0x0021DCF4` directly, including queue completion callbacks and any alternate event-set sites; correlate it with the semaphore completion store before implementing interrupt delivery. Do not signal either event by address. This is static generated-code/source review only; no source behavior or build changed. Milestone 3 and progress bars remain unchanged.

### Candidate producer found in generated NV2A method handling (2026-10-19)

Static review of the generated code found a possible direct producer for the previously blocked queue-space event. The event-setting branch at guest `0x0021701E` in `sub_00216EA0` calls the imported `KeSetEvent` on its method context plus `0x1A4`. That handler is reached through `sub_00217130`, which processes the NV2A context during the guest vector-3 ISR/DPC path. The known vector-3 context base is `0x0021DB50`; adding `0x1A4` yields `0x0021DCF4`, exactly the event object awaited by `sub_00211E10` when the queue fence is full.

This is a stronger candidate than the previously considered DPC signal at `context + 0x194` (event `0x0021DCE4`), and it may explain how the queue wait is intended to resume. Static arithmetic and call-path inspection do not prove that this branch ran, that the actual runtime context was `0x0021DB50` at this call, or that the setter used the expected event handle in the captured stall. Do not alter event behavior based on this inference.

Next: correlate `sub_0021701E` executions and its context pointer with the exact title wait and queue completion during a bounded trace. Then investigate why it is absent or insufficient in the stalled run. No source behavior or build changed; no new runtime test was run. Milestone 3 and progress bars remain unchanged. Preserve the user's uncommitted controller-profile patch.

### DCE4 wait can remain pending (2026-10-19 correction)

A corrected bridge trace captured guest return addresses for five-argument `KeWaitForSingleObject` calls. In a 90-second run, DCE4 (`0x0021DCE4`) had 141 wait entries and 140 successful returns; its final wait remained pending at watchdog expiry. The guest `SignalState` was zero at that wait, and no later DCE4 set was recorded before capture. NV2A USER `PUT=GET=0x00032580`, so this pending wait was not the pushbuffer-full DCF4 case. This corrects the prior conclusion that DCE4 was simply cycling successfully: it returned repeatedly in earlier samples, but can also be the terminal startup wait in another run.

The wait return site was `0x0002DB56`. Generated code maps that to `sub_0002DB50`, which waits through `sub_0020EC60` and then consumes a 16-bit value from a shared ring buffer. Its caller was `0x000DFE7C` in `sub_000DFBC0`, after a sequence of generated graphics/command helper calls. This places the current capture's stall in a ring-buffer consumer path; the exact event producer and why it stopped signaling remain unproven. The separate DCF4 wait and candidate setter at `0x0021701E` were not reached in this run.

Return-address trace caveat: `g_esp+20` produced the verified return site for the five-argument wait bridge. The earlier suggestion to use `g_esp-4` was incorrect. For the three-argument `KeSetEvent` bridge, `g_esp+12` yielded `0xFD000000`, so that set-call return field is invalid; inspect the thunk stack layout before using or reporting KeSetEvent caller addresses.

The 90-second capture reached 343,604 guest indirect calls and watchdog exit code 3. Restored kernel bridge SHA-256 `2190BBD3E20DA13238802F655F198ECEE5BCC8A123CF6A774142C298F47CF232` and generated `recomp_0015.c` from pre-trace copies; rebuilt Release successfully; verified no temporary event or setter markers remain in the executable and no game process remains. ### NV2A nonzero NOP notification handling (2026-10-19)

A bounded 120-second profile recorded 263 nonzero payloads among the first 275 `NV097_NO_OPERATION` methods. The existing pushbuffer executor ignored this method, and the PGRAPH register handler treated interrupt status and enable registers as ordinary storage. The guest's ISR reads the PGRAPH trapped method and notification source; xemu's reference implementation models a nonzero NOP as a PGRAPH error/notification interrupt, records the trapped method/data, and waits for the guest to acknowledge it by writing the interrupt status register ([xemu PGRAPH implementation](https://github.com/xemu-project/xemu/blob/master/hw/xbox/nv2a/nv2a_pgraph.c#L2783-L2875), [xemu NOP notification method](https://github.com/xemu-project/xemu/blob/master/hw/xbox/nv2a/nv2a_pgraph.c#L3268-L3326)).

Implemented that device behavior in the local runtime and saved it as the isolated dependency patch `patches/xboxrecomp-v0.13.1-nv2a-nop-notification.patch`. The Release build succeeded. In a bounded profiled launch, NV2A traces showed the PGRAPH interrupt source asserted and the guest interrupt claimed it; the title art and “PRESS MAIN WEAPON BUTTON” prompt were visible. This verifies the notification/acknowledgement path can run through startup, not that the game is playable.

The same run ended at the 120-second watchdog in the separate queue-space wait on event `0x0021DCF4`, with guest `PUT=0x00080F6C` and `GET=0x0007C52C`. Audio output, a confirmed button response, gameplay, and campaign progression remain unverified. No progress bars advance; Milestone 3 remains active. No game image or generated game code was added to Git.

Next: trace the queue-space producer and the executor's pending segment at the watchdog, including the completion semaphore and the event's set path, then repeat the title-start input with guest-visible controller state.

### DCF4 producer and waiter correlation (2026-10-19)

A bounded 120-second trace recorded 13 waits on queue event `0x0021DCF4`. Twelve returned successfully with `STATUS_SUCCESS`; 12 corresponding `KeSetEvent` calls were observed from guest caller `0x0021702F`, and the guest event state was `1` at each logged return. This shows that the DCF4 event/set/wait bridge can make progress during startup. The last wait had no later return before the watchdog. At capture, the guest was in the DCF4 wait helper (`ESP=0x00F7FEEC`, `ESI=0x0021DCF4`), with NV2A USER `PUT=0x0007FA3C`, `GET=0x0007AFFC`, after 620,126 indirect calls. The trace does not identify why this final producer/completion cycle did not finish; the `KeSetEvent` call site alone is insufficient to prove the intended queue producer was healthy.

Restored `kernel_bridge.c` byte-for-byte to SHA-256 `2190BBD3E20DA13238802F655F198ECEE5BCC8A123CF6A774142C298F47CF232`. A clean Release x64 rebuild completed successfully. The rebuilt executable contains no `[DCF4-*]` trace strings, and no game process remains. The logs and captures are local/ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-dcf4-event-wait-trace-120s-20261019/`. This run did not verify title-screen interaction, audio output, controller response, or gameplay. Progress bars remain unchanged; Milestone 3 remains active.

### DCF4 wait correlated with an in-flight pushbuffer segment (2026-10-19)

A low-overhead 120-second watchdog capture recorded the guest waiting on `0x0021DCF4` while the pushbuffer executor was still scanning a submitted segment. The queue producer was `0x651`, completion was `0x643` (14 entries apart; the helper waits when space falls below its 8-entry threshold), and the DCF4 signal state was zero. NV2A USER `PUT=0x000335D8`, `GET=0x0002A294`. The executor's last completed segment was `0x00025854→0x0002A294`; it was still processing `0x0002A294→0x0002ECEC`. Its last reported cursor was guest `0x8002CCEC`, method `0x17FC` (`SET_BEGIN_END`), count 1, parameter 0. This is consistent with ordinary queue back-pressure while the executor is behind the producer. The snapshot does not prove that method 0x17FC itself is the cause or that the executor was permanently stuck there; it only identifies the method/cursor at watchdog time.

Restored both temporary source traces and rebuilt Release x64 successfully. Verified `xbox_memory_layout.c` returned to baseline SHA-256 `DDBB586B1DBE8B976B23A1662884D1ADF43B89C745684DBF995942E6F57987C3`; verified the rebuilt executable contains no `[PB-STATE]` or `[PB-GUEST]` trace strings and no runtime process remains. The screenshots/logs are ignored locally under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-dcf4-executor-progress-trace-120s-20261019b/`. This does not verify title input, audio output, or gameplay. Progress bars remain unchanged; Milestone 3 remains active.

2026-10-19 XBE thunk mapping for the ring callback: Parsed the user's local XBE kernel thunk table at VA `0x0025D0E0` directly from its section mapping. 2026-10-19 XBE ring producer correlation: Static inspection found that `sub_00216BE0` increments `MEM32(esi + 0x1C0)` then calls the XBE thunk at `0x0025D1E8` with event `esi + 0x194`. The local XBE thunk table maps slot 66 (`0x0025D1E8`) to ordinal 145, `KeSetEvent`; slot 67 (`0x0025D1EC`) is ordinal 159, `KeWaitForSingleObject`. Earlier DCE4 tracing records 7,190 `KeSetEvent` calls from guest return site `0x00216CAE`. For the traced DCE4 event at `0x0021DCE4`, the callback's `esi` base is therefore `0x0021DB50`, making its incremented field `0x0021DD10`. This matches the ring producer field exposed through pointer `0x0021B790 + 0x2580`. This is strong evidence that the device callback publishes the same counter consumed by `sub_0002DB50`, and that DCE4 is its associated notification event. It does not show whether the 25-item consumer request is expected to be fulfilled at that startup stage, nor whether callback scheduling stops or stalls in the intermittent Dolby capture. No runtime source change or test; generated analysis and XBE remain local. M3/progress bars unchanged. Next compare callback invocations, producer count, and the 25-item predicate across short samples in a title-reaching configuration and a Dolby-stalled configuration; do not synthesize event signals.

Several are guarded by device-state bits, and `sub_00216CF0` invokes it after a separate helper call. ### 2026-10-19 crash-site static follow-up

Mapped the keyboard-run crash's guest return address `0x00221331` against the local XBE translation and instruction listing. It returns into `sub_002212F4`, which calls `sub_0021E780`; the latter is a reference-count release helper that reads `[object+4]`, decrements it, and calls the object's first vtable method if the count reaches zero. At failure, the supplied object pointer was `0xFE0001D4`, so the faulting read at `0xFE0001D8` is outside the mapped XBE guest image. The guest stack also includes return address `0x002241EA` in `sub_00224115`, which the translation labels `game_audio` and whose nearby logic traverses audio-related lists and invokes callbacks.

This narrows the observed failure to an invalid guest object pointer during audio/resource cleanup. The producer of that pointer is not known; the stack classification does not prove a DirectSound API mismatch, and the single run does not show that enabling `RECOMP_KEYBOARD` caused it. No fix is justified yet. Next add a capped diagnostic at the release helper/caller to capture the object and list-entry values before the access, then compare matched launches with and without keyboard fallback. ### 2026-10-19 OHCI enabled and title-input report

The previous no-transfer captures had `RECOMP_USB_PROFILE=steel-battalion` but omitted the separate `RECOMP_USB=1` opt-in. `ohci_init` returns before enabling the host controller unless `RECOMP_USB` is set, so the profile variable alone did not start USB input. Relaunched with both flags. OHCI0 became operational after 5.14 seconds, detected the virtual Steel Battalion device on port 1, and completed six XID setup/control requests: device descriptor, address, configuration descriptor, configuration, XID descriptor, and input capabilities. The game then polled 26-byte reports; no control-transfer stall was recorded.

With the title prompt visible, appended a single 500 ms A press through the live-input hook. The report changed from neutral to byte 2 = `0x01`, then returned to neutral. XboxDevWiki's controller-to-Xbox table identifies that field as `RightJoyMainWeapon`; the upstream [Ryzee119 XID test gist](https://gist.github.com/Ryzee119/5b74e94eb765bcc54e68afde9ddc7623) independently defines the Steel Battalion report as 26 bytes. Despite the correctly shaped report and mapped bit, the title prompt did not change. This verifies virtual XID enumeration and report delivery, but not game-side input acceptance or physical controller support. The screenshot remained the title screen at 0 FPS / 29 draws. No gameplay/audio result was verified.

### 2026-10-19 pushbuffer-enabled XID startup

The control-only USB stalls were caused by the diagnostic launch omitting `RECOMP_PB_EXEC=1`. Static inspection and the watchdog's guest registers identify the no-executor spin at `sub_00211E10`'s fence loop: it waits until the completion word at `0x80003000` reaches the caller's target. In the non-executing run, the producer advanced to 11 while the completion word remained 3, so the report endpoint was never initialized. This explains those particular setup-only runs; it is not a controller-protocol failure.

Repeated the bounded run with `RECOMP_PB_EXEC=1`. The guest fence completed (the completion word advanced from 3 to 7), then USB enumeration issued the 26-byte `GET_REPORT` and began periodic XID polling. A 60-second capture served 2,247 periodic reports; a 180-second capture served 10,322. In the longer run, appending a live `a` press changed input-report byte 2 bit 0 to `1`, then returned it to `0`, matching the documented Main Weapon field. This verifies the virtual profile's transport and report mapping under this startup configuration, not game-side input acceptance or a physical controller.

The 180-second run's final framebuffer capture is black; this run does not verify the title screen or a visible response to the input. Keep the earlier title-screen capture as a separate one-run result. The virtual XID progress bar remains 1/3: report delivery is verified; guest state and accepted title action remain unverified. Original Steel Battalion hardware remains 0/3.

Generated `recomp_0015.c` was restored to baseline SHA-256 `2C9B70812633A82ABFC8C2EA2CC2D6B3FA400818385AF341EC73B93ED9BB7867`, the Release x64 executable was rebuilt, diagnostic markers are absent, and no game process remains. The final test logs/screenshots are ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-queue-callback-pbexec-60s-20261019/` and `m3-pbexec-title-input-180s-20261019/`. Use `RECOMP_PB_EXEC=1` for future boot/input diagnostics; without it the guest cannot retire queued GPU work.

### 2026-10-19 byte-verified startup texture cache

Profiled the window-enabled logo draw by stage. The Vulkan submission/setup, queue wait, and readback path took about 8.0–10.2 ms; the first complete draw took 60.6–61.2 ms. The CPU still converted the same 512x512 DXT1 splash texture for every fade update. Added a one-texture decoded-RGBA cache to the existing exact logo path. It is restricted to the observed 512x512 DXT1 format and only reuses decoded pixels after comparing the full 128 KiB guest texture image against a saved copy; a changed offset or changed source bytes triggers a fresh decode. This does not assume guest texture memory is immutable.

A 30-second window-enabled run logged one cache refresh and four subsequent byte-verified hits. Later logo draws measured 34.3–35.7 ms, versus 61.2 ms for the first decode in the same run (about 42% lower). The output uses the same cached conversion of unchanged source bytes; the Vulkan submission path itself is unchanged. The 25-second guest watchdog still did not reach the Steel Battalion title screen, and this startup sample is not a gameplay or steady-state frame-rate benchmark. No progress bars advance.

The reusable change is isolated in `patches/xboxrecomp-v0.13.1-vulkan-logo-texture-cache.patch`. Verified that the patch applies to the saved post-Vulkan-logo baseline and reproduces the local modified source hash. Removed temporary timing code from the framebuffer presenter, restored the kernel source except for the cache change, rebuilt the Release x64 target successfully, verified no temporary gate/timing markers remain in the executable, and confirmed no game process remains. User's modified XID profile patch is untouched.

Next profile the remaining roughly 27 ms between the total draw and measured Vulkan stage, including time waiting for the presenter queue lock. Then measure and improve the per-draw surface readback/serialization without changing guest timing or raster semantics. Keep Milestone 3 active; repeatable title boot, audio, accepted guest input, original controller hardware, and campaign remain unverified.

### 2026-10-20 windowed startup presentation handoff

Rebuilt the current `build-xpp-seeded` Release x64 target; an initial diagnostic had accidentally used the older October 17 `build/Release` binary and its black-frame result was discarded. A matched windowed run with the current executable reached 95 flips and captured the Nude Maker startup logo in the actual presenter output at 640x480. A traced run showed the completed guest framebuffer alternates between `0x80084000` and `0x801B0000`; logo-stage frames contained about 11,000–13,000 nonblack pixels at the present callback, and those pixels were copied into the window thread's upload buffer. The callback can advance twice before a UI poll, returning to the same alternating buffer index. The old index-only change test could then mistake a new frame for the already shown one.

Added an isolated follow-up patch, `patches/xboxrecomp-v0.13.1-vulkan-frame-generation.patch`, based on the existing Vulkan framebuffer presenter patch. It serializes the guest-to-host frame copy with an SRW lock and tracks an interlocked generation counter, so a reused buffer index still signals a fresh frame. The patch passes `git apply --check` against the saved post-presenter baseline. The current Release x64 target rebuilt successfully with the trace-only probes removed. The matched 36-second watchdog run captured the Nude Maker logo and 95 flips; a separate later capture ended on a black frame after the startup stage. That later frame does not establish title-screen boot or a stable running scene.

This is a verified startup-logo presentation result only. The game did not reach its title prompt in the matched run. Audio output, accepted guest input, gameplay, saves, and campaign progress remain unverified. No performance bar or milestone bar advances; Milestone 3 remains active. User's modified Steel Battalion XID profile patch was not touched. Logs and captures remain local/ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-*-20261020/`.

### 2026-10-20 guest XID payload-state trace

Followed the completion path downstream from `sub_0025BFA3` through its dynamically selected callback `sub_00259A31` to target `0x0025D059`. That routine reads the report length, copies the 24-byte body from context offset `+0x34` to the guest state at `+0x14` (removing the two-byte XID header), then checks the first three 16-bit button words. Its nonzero path tail-calls `sub_00193C94`; this call relationship is established from static control flow, not a separately captured runtime entry.

A temporary trace sampled the post-copy guest buffer at `0x010816A5` during the scripted A press/release. The first button word changed from `0x0000` to `0x0001` while held and returned to `0x0000` after release. The aiming axes were centered (`00 80`). XboxDevWiki's Steel Battalion controller-to-Xbox table maps report byte 2 bit 0 to `RightJoyMainWeapon`, confirming the test bit's documented meaning ([Xbox Input Devices](https://xboxdevwiki.net/Xbox_Input_Devices)). This verifies report delivery into the game's guest-side input state. It does not verify that `sub_00193C94` executes dynamically, that the title prompt accepts the action, or that physical hardware is supported.

The bounded 45-second sample still rendered only one flip at 0.00 FPS; the watchdog found no guest return with NV2A USER `PUT=GET=0x495C`. It did not reproduce the title prompt and is not campaign or gameplay evidence. After collecting it, all temporary traces were restored. Baseline hashes: `recomp_0017.c` `DE5C51273DA352EB863775DE2DC387C6CCFE9426A671C36BF068EBE57FEB8F15`; `recomp_0018.c` `77071AF8047D254E70A10A1E7F0A9909F1C44C0A62FAE94F5DDB5ADB1E94549B`; OHCI source `52C4E93766A84962803BD4E963FD24D79041C94F483707F8CC827172FDA257A5`. A forced Release rebuild completed and the final executable contains none of the temporary trace markers. No game process remains; captures and logs are ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-xid-dispatch-trace-20261020/`.

The virtual XID path is only partially verified: report mapping and packet changes are observed, and one scripted sample changed the guest button word; a title-synchronized A press changed the USB report but left the callback source and copied guest button words at zero, with the prompt unchanged. Treat guest input delivery as inconsistent and unverified until the USB transfer buffer and callback are correlated in the same title run. The overall project remains at milestone 2/8, with Milestone 3 active. Audio, gameplay, saves, full campaign, and original controller hardware remain unverified.

### 2026-10-20 kernel callback stack/register preservation

Compared the pinned XboxRecomp v0.13.1 runtime with the upstream callback fix ([commit 117a649](https://github.com/sp00nznet/xboxrecomp/commit/117a6498047c6ab93a48073875a071bff084c9a7)). The local `deliver_one_apc` called the guest completion routine and then advanced guest ESP by a fixed 12 bytes, even though the callback's stdcall return had already consumed its return address and arguments. Reproduced this with upstream's focused inline I/O APC test: before the fix, `NtReadFile` returned at ESP `0x00020030` instead of expected `0x00020024` (a 12-byte overrun). The same upstream fix also preserves the caller's register globals around APC, DPC, `NtUserIoApcDispatcher`, and `KeSynchronizeExecution` callbacks, which run inline on the caller's recompiled thread.

Ported the focused change and adapted the upstream `kernel_io_apc` test for the v0.13.1 API (which does not yet expose deferred APC support). The test intentionally clobbers the callback register globals and verifies both caller-register preservation and exact post-`NtReadFile` ESP. It passed all checks after the fix. The full Steel Battalion Release x64 executable rebuilt successfully with the patched kernel bridge. The isolated patch is `patches/xboxrecomp-v0.13.1-kernel-callback-register-preservation.patch`; it applies cleanly in reverse to the modified local dependency, and the test source is included in the patch. XboxRecomp's MIT license was reviewed; no license conflict was found.

A matched windowed startup using the same service/profile flags as the preceding no-fix retry now progressed farther: 321 flips, loaded `advdat00.adv`, and visibly reached the Steel Battalion title prompt. The preceding no-fix windowed run reached 172 flips and did not load that title asset. This is evidence that the callback correction may fix a startup progression fault, not proof of causality or reliable boot; a repeat is still needed. After an `a` was appended to the live input file while the prompt was visible, the prompt remained, and this run did not enable report tracing, so XID delivery or game-side input acceptance is not verified. At the 180-second watchdog the guest was waiting on title event `0x0021DCF4` (`ESI=0x0021DCF4`); NV2A USER PUT=GET=`0x50E8`. The display stopped refreshing at 321 flips (0.00 FPS). Logs and captures are ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-xid-apc-fix-windowed-20261020/`.

The dependency patch is backed by a failing-before/passing-after regression and a successful full game build. No game process remains; the user's controller-profile patch is untouched and unstaged. Progress bars remain unchanged, Milestone 3 stays active. Next repeat the patched windowed boot, add a targeted XID report/state trace after the visible prompt, then investigate why the title event wait and frame updates stop. Audio output, playable input, gameplay, saving, and campaign progression remain unverified.

### 2026-10-20 upstream scheduler research and bounded guest-lock probe

Fetched upstream XboxRecomp tags through v0.15.0 and reviewed its MIT-licensed scheduler and translator changes. The most relevant upstream work is the fair one-guest-CPU scheduler with a 4 ms quantum, a translator `--back-edge-yield` option that checks at guest loop back-edges, and a DISPATCH/interrupt gate. These additions are documented in [upstream commit c7a1817](https://github.com/sp00nznet/xboxrecomp/commit/c7a1817) and [scheduler commit 5bb9e04](https://github.com/sp00nznet/xboxrecomp/commit/5bb9e04). The current local `RECOMP_GUEST_LOCK` is only a critical section and has no back-edge preemption. The upstream generator patch does not apply cleanly to the locally extended translator, so no bulk upgrade or upstream source change was applied.

The restored ordinary Release target rebuilt through the Visual Studio 2022 bundled CMake successfully. A bounded run with the existing lock enabled rendered only one flip, stayed CPU-busy, never loaded `advdat00.adv`, and produced no watchdog report before the exact diagnostic process was stopped. This is consistent with the upstream warning that a lock without guest loop preemption can deadlock; it does not diagnose the normal unlocked title stall. The rebuilt executable contains none of the temporary raw-watchdog markers, and no game process remains.

Next: adapt the upstream fair scheduler and loop-back-edge preemption as an isolated runtime patch, preserve the current title-specific Vulkan/APU/USB changes, then compare matched boot runs with the optional scheduler disabled and enabled. ### 2026-10-19 scheduler adaptation and boot-fence diagnosis

Read the upstream XboxRecomp v0.15.0 fair guest-CPU scheduler and `--back-edge-yield` implementation, then adapted the relevant pieces locally without changing the default execution path. Full generation completed with 9,400 translated units and zero translator failures; 7,901 backward guest branches contain the optional yield hook. A 250-function source split stalled MSVC in one 37 MB generated C file, so the diagnostic candidate was regenerated with 50 functions per source and built with parallel compilation. The Release x64 candidate linked successfully with a local PDB and map file. All generated game code and build output remain ignored.

Matched 40-second startup runs with scheduler enabled and disabled stopped at the same guest state. Scheduler counters were zero preemptions, zero waiters, zero switches, and one kernel release; the guest was waiting for the NV2A completion word at `0x80003000` to advance from 3 to 9. This rules out the scheduler as the cause of that wait. With `RECOMP_PB_EXEC=1`, the executor completed semaphore releases 5, 7, and 9, allowing startup to enter audio initialization.

With synthetic AC'97 readiness and the diagnostic `RECOMP_APU_DSP_ACK=0x803B8810`, a 90-second bounded run acknowledged DSP doorbell command 3, loaded title sound-bank assets, reached three flips and four draw calls (16 indices, 1,228,132 pixels written), and then remained in the slow title/resource path. A follow-up 180-second capture stayed at the same three flips/four draws, showed 0.00 FPS in repeated samples, and reported zero OHCI periodic transfers. Its watchdog again sampled `sub_0020F2C0` in the resource-helper path; this is a sampled location, not proof that the helper itself is defective. No recognizable title screen, periodic USB report, audible DSP output, game input acceptance, gameplay, save/load, or campaign progression was verified. Synthetic DSP acknowledgement is diagnostic only and does not establish DSP execution or audio correctness. No progress bars advance; Milestone 3 remains active.

Diagnostic logs are ignored under `iso/local-analysis/logs/m3-guest-cpu-scheduler-20261019/`, `m3-native-pc-20261019/`, `m3-pushbuffer-boot-20261019/`, and `m3-pushbuffer-dsp-ack-20261019/`. The isolated candidate includes temporary generated-source tracing and must not replace the ordinary build. No game or emulator process remains.

Next trace the resource-loading path and the first post-audio wait using the PDB-enabled diagnostic build, while keeping synthetic DSP acknowledgement separate from any audio compatibility claim. Preserve the default scheduler-off behavior until a test demonstrates that scheduling resolves a measured race or boot issue.

Follow-up source inspection narrowed the sampled frame: `sub_0020F2C0` is a short helper that reads state at `0x21AFE0` and writes two result words; its caller `sub_0002DB50` loops while comparing guest counters at `0x355730` and `0x351F5C`. The 180-second native stack had that caller directly beneath the helper. This makes the repeated counter-wait loop the actionable trace point, rather than treating the helper as a slow resource routine. Next capture those counters, the helper's returned state, and the worker-side writes during the stall; whether this is a missing completion or an expected wait is still unverified.

### 2026-10-20 guest-event wait diagnosis and extended startup capture

The counter trace revised the prior diagnosis. At the 90-second sample, the guest repeatedly checked `request=1`, `observed=1`, `base=1`; the kernel callback trace identified the preceding indirect call as slot 67 remapped to `KeWaitForSingleObject` (ordinal 159), on the title-built event at `0x0021DCE4`. With `RECOMP_TITLE_KEVENTS` omitted, the bridge passed this guest address to Win32 as a native handle and returned `STATUS_UNSUCCESSFUL` (`0xC0000001`), leaving the guest to spin. Enabling the existing title-event bridge created a host shadow for the guest event, and the same wait returned `STATUS_SUCCESS` with guest signal state 1. Subsequent progress sampling showed the event-backed counter advancing (for example, observed `0x882` against base `0x85B` for request 1). This identifies a configuration-sensitive immediate cause; it does not establish that the title-event bridge is safe as a default for every title.

### 2026-10-20 guest input state narrowed to title-side polling

The guest callback trace showed a stronger boundary than the preceding USB endpoint trace: the 26-byte XID report's payload byte 0 changes to `0x01` while the mapped Main Weapon control is pressed, is copied from the callback source into the guest device state, then returns to `0x00` on release. Static analysis of `sub_0025D059` shows it scans the copied report words and branches to `sub_00193C94` when a nonzero input is present. `sub_00193C94` calls imported kernel ordinal 149 (`KeSetTimer`) for guest timer `0x006ED4E0` with DPC `0x006ED4C0`; its DPC is initialized to `sub_00193CAF`, which checks guest state and can call ordinal 360 (`HalInitiateShutdown`). This callback path is therefore a device/report event and deferred timer path, not direct evidence of title prompt action dispatch.

The independent guest-memory probe verifies the report bit is copied, but the final title screen did not visibly react to scripted presses. Do not change the mapping yet. Next follow the device-state object into the title's polling routine and prompt transition code, and trace the timer/DPC only if that path depends on it. At a reported sample the renderer had presented 98 flips at roughly 0.81 FPS, completed 194 draw submissions, and NV2A DMA PUT equaled GET (`0x2769C`).
